[Director 指令] [Patch] 数据审计修正 — 4 项确认错误

## 1. [CRITICAL] Attacked K_eff 表格修正 (C2)

appendix.tex 中 Attacked K_eff Stability 表格，以下 3 个值需修正（position_bias MMLU/ARC 看起来被交换了）：

完整正确表格值：
| Attack | MMLU ρ | ARC ρ |
|--------|--------|-------|
| prompt_injection | 0.63 | 0.60 |
| sycophancy | 0.91 | 0.92 |
| score_manipulation | 0.98 | 0.99 |
| verbosity_bias | 0.92 | 0.92 |
| position_bias | **0.60** | **0.08** |
| authority_bias | 0.95 | 0.97 |

注意：position_bias 的行最关键——MMLU 应为 0.60（非 0.42），ARC 应为 0.08（非 0.65）。analysis.tex 正文的 "ρ=0.08 on ARC" 是正确的。

同时更新 analysis.tex 中 "4/6 attacks stable (ρ>0.91)" 的表述：
- 按正确数据，ρ>0.90 的 attacks: sycophancy, score_manipulation, verbosity_bias, authority_bias = 4 个 ✅
- prompt_injection ρ≈0.60-0.63 = moderate
- position_bias ρ=0.08/0.60 = 不稳定
- 所以 "4/6 stable" 大致成立（如果 "stable" 定义为 ρ>0.90），但要确保与表格数据一致

## 2. [MEDIUM] Capability-matched r 值修正 (C3)

论文三处 r=0.41 改为 r=0.50：
- appendix.tex Table 7 (robustness checks): "0.41" → "0.50"
- appendix.tex text: "$r{=}0.41$" → "$r{=}0.50$"
- analysis.tex: "$r{=}0.41$" → "$r{=}0.50$"

叙事不变（r=0.50 仍远低于 full pool r=0.70，collinearity 大幅降低），只是数值修正。

## 3. [MINOR] Random subsample 峰值修正 (C4)

appendix.tex random subsample 段中 "n_local=4-5" 改为 "n_local=3-4"。

## 4. [CRITICAL] η_max OLS 显著性计数 (C5)

Agent 确认 OLS FDR 显著 η_max 为 **9/12**（非 11/12）。不显著的 3 条件：mmlu×score_manip (p=0.314), arc×score_manip (p=0.212), arc×position_bias (p=0.445)。

搜索论文中所有出现 "11/12" 且与 η_max OLS 相关的位置，修正为 "9/12"。注意：
- **Bootstrap 计数不受影响**（K=3 η_max 5/12 仍然正确）
- 只有 OLS 表格和 OLS 引用需要修改
- Correction gradient（OLS → CR1 → bootstrap）中的 OLS 行需要从 11/12 改为 9/12
- 如果 introduction 或 其他章节引用了 OLS 11/12，也需要修改

paper_framing.md 中的 11/12 由我来修正，你只负责 .tex 文件。

完成后编译验证，报告页数是否仍为 8 页主文。

```text
126	The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) demonstrates that elevated collinearity between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ partially inflates standard errors: both predictors achieve moderate significance but neither dominates, and the transition does not replicate. Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.41$) strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the transition weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 4--5 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
127	
128	\section{Random Subsample Analysis}
129	\label{app:random_subsample}
130	
131	To test whether the regime transition depends on model pool composition, we randomly sample 9 of 15 models (1{,}000 trials), construct all $\binom{9}{5}$ panels, and run the bootstrap analysis at $K{=}5$. We stratify by the number of local models ($n_{\mathrm{local}} \in \{0, \ldots, 6\}$) in each subsample.
132	
133	Under clustered standard errors, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the dominant predictor in 51.4\% of subsamples (mean significance 1.85/12), with an inverted-U as a function of $n_{\mathrm{local}}$: subsamples with 4--5 local models (maximizing capability range) achieve the highest $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance rate, while all-API ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{=}0$) and heavily-local ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{\geq}5$) subsamples show reduced detection. Significance is attenuated relative to the full pool by smaller sample sizes (126 vs.\ 3{,}003 panels at $K{=}5$), but the transition direction holds in the majority of subsamples. This confirms that the transition's detectability depends on capability diversity in the model pool rather than pool size per se.
134	
135	\section{Attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability}
136	\label{app:attacked_keff}
137	
138	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean (unattacked) pairwise MI (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), but used to predict vulnerability under attack. We verify this assumption by recomputing MI from judge outputs under each attack type and comparing clean vs.\ attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ via Spearman rank correlation across all panels.
139	
```

Dom.\ column: $\eta$ = $\eta_{\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \citep{budescu1993dominance}.}

```text
34	
35	$\eta_{\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 11/12 conditions (sole exception: ARC $\times$ position bias, $p = 0.909$), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 4 $\eta_{\max}$-dominant, 7 co-dominant, and 1 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.
36	
37	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Table~\ref{tab:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections.
38

Mixed effects uses 3-position max-$p$ with random judge intercepts. Wild cluster bootstrap uses $B{=}9999$ resamples, Rademacher weights, and max-$p$ across 3 panel positions. $\eta$:$K$ ratio = $\eta_{\max}$ significant conditions / $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant conditions.}
53	\label{tab:robustness}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	$\eta_{\max}$ remains robust: 8/12 under clustered SE, 7/12 under mixed effects, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap (Figure~\ref{fig:robustness}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses under correction: OLS's 8/12 reduces to 0/12 (clustered SE) and 1/12 (bootstrap). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
```

```text
155	\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\rho{>}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation; position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}
156	\label{tab:attacked_keff}

158	
159	Four attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\rho{>}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.61$), consistent with its mechanism of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\eta_{\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Q5mj4CaEwxsjxPxMjPf4JP`</summary>

```text
27	\label{app:bft_reversal}
28	
29	Under standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\eta_{\max}$ significant in 11 of 12 conditions and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in 7. Non-independence correction reveals an asymmetry: $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 5 to 8 of 12 conditions depending on correction method (clustered SE: 8/12; mixed effects: 7/12; wild cluster bootstrap: 5/12), while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses to 0--1/12 (clustered SE 0/12; bootstrap 1/12; mixed effects 3/12). The directional pattern (point estimates predominantly positive) persists, but statistical confidence is limited. The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship that becomes artifactual under adaptive attack (0--1/12 after correction).
30	
31	Under OLS, the sole negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient is ARC $\times$ sycophancy ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$, FDR $p = 0.013$), though this effect does not survive non-independence correction (clustered $p = 0.441$). The GAM taxonomy subsumes this individual exception: the directional pattern across all 6 shared-bias conditions is the stronger evidence, not any single condition's significance.
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "5794a382-4242-4789-b22c-49e7bc8c14f6",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "74f9a774-7a8a-4fd8-9126-5e65f6d78ed0",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "cac9f277-ac6f-46b1-8295-6fba308790af",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.41$)",
    "newString": "capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.50$)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may present more distinct attack surfaces, consistent with the observed positive coefficient, while low-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels share robust consensus. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.41$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{>}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",

          " ",
```

```json
    "newString": "recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may present more distinct attack surfaces, consistent with the observed positive coefficient, while low-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels share robust consensus. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{>}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",
```

```text
105	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
106	
107	GAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions, consistent with diverse panels exposing more distinct weaknesses. \textit{Shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6, consistent with diverse panels diluting shared susceptibility. At $K{=}5$, the split is largely preserved (7:5): one shared-bias condition (MMLU $\times$ sycophancy) flips positive, but the remaining 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.
108	
109	\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}
```

</details>

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating Paper Agent to revise an EMNLP 2026 (ARR) paper "The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels." This session covers multiple patch rounds:
   - **Sensitivity contour figure**: Add K=5 contour plot to appendix §Cinelli-Hazlett
   - **Cross-benchmark prediction**: New appendix section showing MMLU→ARC K_eff prediction (14/18 bootstrap)
   - **Em-dash cleanup**: Remove all 6 prose em-dashes, sync abstract.tex, remove "Notably"
   - **Cinelli-Hazlett data correction (CRITICAL)**: Replace fake sensitivity table with real data from JSON extraction
   - **K=5 aggregation table**: Add bootstrap summary table supporting Bayesian neutralization claim
   - **Page count verification**: Confirm 8-page main text compliance
   - **4-item data audit (CRITICAL)**: Fix attacked K_eff values, capability-matched r, subsample peak, η_max OLS count
   - **Abstract number density**: Reduce from 12+ to 3-4 key numbers
   - **Figure 1 position check**: Verify framework figure on pages 1-2

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - K-dependent vulnerability regime transition: K=3 η_max dominates (5/12), K≥5 K_eff dominates (7/12)
   - Cinelli-Hazlett omitted variable bias framework: RV (robustness value) ranges 0.005–0.522
   - η_max exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions (K=3: 11/12, K=5: 9/12, K=7: 8/12)
   - Cross-benchmark K_eff prediction: MMLU→ARC 14/18, ARC→MMLU 10/18, r≈0.76-0.78
   - Attacked K_eff stability: 4/6 attacks stable (ρ≥0.91), prompt injection moderate (ρ≈0.60-0.63), position bias disrupted (ρ=0.08 ARC)
   - Capability-matched subsample: r=0.50 (corrected from 0.41), strengthens K_eff to 9/12
   - η_max OLS significance: 9/12 (corrected from 11/12), non-significant in MMLU×score_manip, ARC×score_manip, ARC×position_bias
   - Bayesian calibration: reverses K_eff at K=3 (6/7 negative), neutralizes at K=5 (η 1/12, K_eff 0/12)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited references/appendix, Limitations doesn't count
   - Wild cluster bootstrap with K-position max-p, BH FDR α=0.05
   - Paper Agent communicates via `paper_agent_post_to_director` (NOT `main_agent_post_to_director`)

```json
        "content": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may present more distinct attack surfaces, consistent with the observed positive coefficient, while low-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels share robust consensus. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & n.s.    & -- \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $-^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\n$\\eta_{\\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation, ARC $\\times$ score manipulation, and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 3 $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant, 6 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 1 condition where neither predictor reaches significance. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Table~\\ref{tab:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{dir.} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta}$\\textbf{:}$\\boldsymbol{K}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                   & 9/12  & 8/12 & 7+/1$-$ & 1.1:1 \\\\\nClustered SE (CR1)    & 8/12  & 0/12 & ---     & ---   \\\\\nMixed effects         & 7/12  & 3/12 & 3+/0$-$ & 2.3:1 \\\\\nWild cluster bootstrap & 5/12  & 1/12 & 1+/0$-$ & 5:1   \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Robustness of regression results to non-independence correction ($K{=}3$). Clustered SE uses 3-position max-SE with CR1 correction (13 clusters per position). Mixed effects uses 3-position max-$p$ with random judge intercepts. Wild cluster bootstrap uses $B{=}9999$ resamples, Rademacher weights, and max-$p$ across 3 panel positions. $\\eta$:$K$ ratio = $\\eta_{\\max}$ significant conditions / $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant conditions.}\n\\label{tab:robustness}\n\\end{table}\n\n$\\eta_{\\max}$ remains robust: 8/12 under clustered SE, 7/12 under mixed effects, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap (Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses under correction: OLS's 8/12 reduces to 0/12 (clustered SE) and 1/12 (bootstrap). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis (1{,}000 iterations, 13 clusters per position) yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power, below the OLS estimate of 0.164. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \\emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD. The sole negative coefficient (ARC $\\times$ sycophancy, $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$) does not survive correction (bootstrap $p \\approx 0.06$). FDR counts are stable across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ results above show that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ fails to reach significance under corrected inference. A natural question is whether this negligibility is intrinsic to judge diversity or an artifact of small panel size. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{dir.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 455     & 5/12 & 1/12 & 1+/0$-$ \\\\\n5 & 3{,}003 & 0/12 & 7/12 & 7+/0$-$ \\\\\n7 & 6{,}435 & 0/12 & 5/12 & 5+/0$-$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-$K$ wild cluster bootstrap summary ($B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, $K$-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$, $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$). As panel size increases, $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance drops from 5/12 to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises from 1/12 to 7/12. All significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients are positive.}\n\\label{tab:cross_k}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_k} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12 significant) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, this pattern reverses: $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. The transition persists at $K{=}7$ (0/12, 5/12). The $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient does not shift from positive to negative across $K$; it is positive in every significant condition at every panel size. Instead, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ transitions from a negligible to a significant positive predictor of panel vulnerability at the aggregate level.\n\nAll significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients are positive across all $K$ values. The sole negative outlier (ARC $\\times$ sycophancy) does not reach FDR significance at any $K \\geq 5$. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ bootstrap-significant in 2/2 conditions ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = +0.215$ and $+0.379$); under adaptive attack, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant), while adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256, but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significant predictive power (7/12). The random-to-adaptive gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming that the contrast is most pronounced in small panels where $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \\textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions, consistent with diverse panels exposing more distinct weaknesses. \\textit{Shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6, consistent with diverse panels diluting shared susceptibility. At $K{=}5$, the split is largely preserved (7:5): one shared-bias condition (MMLU $\\times$ sycophancy) flips positive, but the remaining 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\\\\nMajority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Figure in Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity ($t{=}K$) makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge, inflating predictability. Bayesian calibration reverses the diversity channel: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction flips from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).\n",

        "totalLines": 133
      }
```

```text
135	\section{Attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability}
136	\label{app:attacked_keff}
137	
138	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean (unattacked) pairwise MI (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), but used to predict vulnerability under attack. We verify this assumption by recomputing MI from judge outputs under each attack type and comparing clean vs.\ attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ via Spearman rank correlation across all panels.
139	
140	\begin{table}[h]
141	\centering
142	\small
143	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
144	\toprule
145	\textbf{Attack} & \textbf{MMLU $\rho$} & \textbf{ARC $\rho$} \\
146	\midrule
147	Score manipulation & 0.98 & 0.99 \\
148	Authority bias     & 0.95 & 0.97 \\
149	Sycophancy         & 0.91 & 0.92 \\
150	Verbosity bias     & 0.92 & 0.92 \\
151	Prompt injection   & 0.63 & 0.60 \\
152	Position bias      & 0.60 & 0.08 \\
153	\bottomrule
154	\end{tabular}
155	\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\rho{\geq}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$); position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}
156	\label{tab:attacked_keff}
157	\end{table}
158	
159	Four attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\rho{\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mechanism of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\eta_{\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.
160	
161	\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}
162	\label{app:sensitivity}
163	
164	We apply the Cinelli--Hazlett omitted variable bias framework to quantify how robust $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is to unobserved confounders. For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \times 2$ benchmarks $\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have, in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome, to reduce $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Attacked $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability}\n\\label{app:attacked_keff}\n\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean (unattacked) pairwise MI (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), but used to predict vulnerability under attack. We verify this assumption by recomputing MI from judge outputs under each attack type and comparing clean vs.\\ attacked $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ via Spearman rank correlation across all panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{MMLU $\\rho$} & \\textbf{ARC $\\rho$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nScore manipulation & 0.98 & 0.99 \\\\\nAuthority bias     & 0.95 & 0.97 \\\\\nSycophancy         & 0.91 & 0.92 \\\\\nVerbosity bias     & 0.92 & 0.92 \\\\\nPrompt injection   & 0.63 & 0.60 \\\\\nPosition bias      & 0.60 & 0.08 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\\rho{\\approx}0.60$--$0.63$); position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}\n\\label{tab:attacked_keff}\n\\end{table}\n\nFour attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\\rho{\\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mechanism of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\\eta_{\\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.\n\n\\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:sensitivity}\n\nWe apply the Cinelli--Hazlett omitted variable bias framework to quantify how robust $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is to unobserved confounders. For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \\times 2$ benchmarks $\\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have, in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome, to reduce $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{rcllcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Data} & \\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{RV} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta}$ $\\boldsymbol{R^2_{\\mathrm{tr}}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta}$ $\\boldsymbol{R^2_{\\mathrm{out}}}$ & \\textbf{Exc.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & MMLU & Prompt inj.    & 0.419 & 0.587 & 0.059 & -- \\\\",
```

```json
  "content": "[Director 指令] **[Patch] R3 审稿响应 — 17 条 issue 整批修复**\n\n目标：EMNLP 2026 ARR Long Paper。当前 3.5 分，目标 4.0。正文严格 8 页（当前溢出 1.5 行到第 9 页）。本轮引用需要验证。\n\n所有 evidence 数据已由 Agent 计算完毕，存放在 `docs/paper/data/` 目录。以下逐条修改指令，按优先级排列。**页数管理是硬约束**——增加内容的同时必须压缩其他部分，保持正文 ≤8 页。\n\n---\n\n## CRITICAL (3 条)\n\n### R1-N1: K_eff 独立性 — Pool A evidence + reframe\n\n**数据源**: `docs/paper/data/sensitivity_analysis_poolA.json`\n\nPool A (9 models, r=0.50) Cinelli-Hazlett 结果：\n- K=3: rv_exceeds 3/12 (vs full pool 1/12)\n- K=5: rv_exceeds 6/12 (vs full pool 3/12)\n- K=5 改善集中在 ARC (5/6 exceeds)，MMLU 改善有限 (1/6，且该条件 R²≈0)\n- 保守计数建议 5/12（排除 mmlu×authority R²≈0 的条件）\n\n修改要求：\n1. **§5.2 或 §6 加入 Pool A 关键数字**（2-3 句话）：capability-matched subsample (r=0.50, 9 models, 126 panels at 1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}
```

```text
15	\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}

17	\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}
18	
19	\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}
20	
21	\author{Anonymous}
22	
23	\begin{document}
24	\maketitle
25	
26	\begin{abstract}
27	Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing inspiration from fault tolerance in distributed systems. We decompose panel vulnerability into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). We discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition: $\eta_{\max}$ dominates at small panels, but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ emerges as the dominant predictor at $K{\geq}5$ (bootstrap significance rising from 1/12 to 7/12). This aggregate positive association masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel and neutralizing both predictors at larger panels. The defense strategy must match the panel size regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence, with the net effect depending on attack type and aggregation method.
28	\end{abstract}
29	
30	\input{introduction}
31	\input{related_work}
32	\input{threat_model}
33	\input{methodology}
34	\input{results}
35	\input{analysis}
36	\input{conclusion}
37	
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$ ($\binom{15}{3}{=}455$ to $\binom{15}{7}{=}6{,}435$ panels). We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$ (7 positions vs.\ 3), which may partly explain the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice question formats; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which reduces judge accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:models}). Generalization to open-ended generation evaluation and full-precision deployments remains untested. Third, we study only three discrete $K$ values; the transition threshold between the $\eta_{\max}$-dominant and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regimes is bounded to $3 < K^* < 5$ but not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent: it requires sufficient capability diversity ($r(K_{\mathrm{eff}}, \eta_{\max}) \lesssim 0.60$) to manifest, and is strongest when the pool spans multiple capability tiers. Training-based diversity control \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving} could enable causal identification in future work.
41	
42	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
43	Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.
44	
45	\input{appendix}
46	\input{appendix_attacks}
47	
48	\bibliography{references}
49	
50	\end{document}
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\S\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\eta_{\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}).}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.
11	
12	Yet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \citep{schneier2000secrets}.
13	
14	In our experiments, we discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ dominates; at $K{\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\eta_{\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration reverses the direction entirely.
15	
16	Controlled experiments across 15 models, $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}). Wild cluster bootstrap shows $\eta_{\max}$: 5/12 $\to$ 0/12 $\to$ 0/12 across $K$, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$: 1/12 $\to$ 7/12 $\to$ 5/12. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$, largely preserved at $K{=}5$. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.
17	
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} $\eta_{\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); reversal at $K{\geq}5$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12, $\eta_{\max}$ 0/12). The transition requires model pools with sufficient capability diversity to manifest; under random faults, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ surviving all corrections, with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
21	\item \textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
22	\item \textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. Wild cluster bootstrap shows $\\eta_{\\max}$: 5/12 $\\to$ 0/12 $\\to$ 0/12 across $K$, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$: 1/12 $\\to$ 7/12 $\\to$ 5/12. GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$, largely preserved at $K{=}5$. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}
4	LLM judges gained traction after \citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \citet{shi2024judgerobust} further quantified robustness gaps under adversarial prompts. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \citet{dawid1979maximum}; \citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\% to 10--19\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness.
5	
6	\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}
7	\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5. \citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee; \citet{kaniovski2011optimal} derived optimal jury size under correlated votes. LLM judge outputs exhibit precisely this correlation structure, and our results instantiate the CJT failure mode: when adaptive attacks push judges below the critical threshold, panel expansion amplifies error.
8	
9	\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}
10	\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance collapses after correction (8/12$\to$1/12); at $K{\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.
11	
12	\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}
13	Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.
14	
15	\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}
16	Classical BFT \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition.
17	
18	\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}
19	Information-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.
20	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	The dominant vulnerability predictor in LLM judge panels transitions with panel size: $\eta_{\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (7/12, $\eta_{\max}$ 0/12). The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ association is attack-mechanism-dependent (targeted positive, shared-bias negative); Bayesian calibration reverses it at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$. Observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). For deployment: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual robustness and use Bayesian aggregation; at $K{\geq}5$, prefer correlated robustness profiles over maximally independent judges. Causal identification requires training-based diversity control \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}; the transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) and generalization beyond multiple-choice formats remain open.
4	
```

```text
1	\section{Experimental Methodology}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}
5	
6	We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\,GB). API models are assigned to \emph{frontier}, \emph{mid-tier}, or \emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\times$ item $\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \S\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\begin{tabular}{lllccc}
12	\toprule
13	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Tier} & \textbf{Deploy} & \textbf{MMLU} & \textbf{ARC} \\
14	\midrule
15	gpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\
16	gemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\
17	claude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\
18	\addlinespace
19	gemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & mid-tier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\dagger$ \\
20	gpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\
21	claude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\
22	\addlinespace
23	gpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\
24	gpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\
25	claude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\
26	mistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\
27	llama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\
28	llama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\
29	qwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\
30	qwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\
31	qwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\dagger$\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\% format error rate.}
35	\label{tab:models}
36	\end{table}
37	
38	\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}
39	
40	Two benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each benchmark. MMLU probes factual recall and broad domain knowledge; ARC requires multi-step scientific reasoning. All API calls use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs; local models are served with the same temperature setting. Attacked variants are generated by programmatically applying each manipulation to the candidate text, with a fixed random seed for reproducibility.
41	
42	Each benchmark is paired with six attack types that target different judge vulnerabilities: (1)~\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions embedded in candidate text that override the evaluation prompt \citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\textbf{sycophancy}, responses engineered to agree with or flatter the judge \citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues (e.g., ``this answer deserves a 10/10'') injected into the candidate to anchor scoring; (4)~\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding correct answers with excessive irrelevant detail to exploit length preferences \citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\textbf{position bias}, reordering candidate answers to place the target in the position judges systematically favor \citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\textbf{authority bias}, prefacing candidate text with fabricated credentials or citations to inflate perceived credibility. The combination of 2 benchmarks and 6 attacks produces 12 experimental conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\ref{app:attacks}).
43	
44	\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}
45	
46	Per-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\eta_{\max}(a) = \max_{j \in \mathcal{P}} \eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise mutual information between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean (unattacked) data. MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. In practice, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed through the Gaussian copula correlation matrix: under the Gaussian copula assumption (that pairwise dependence is adequately captured by rank correlations), pairwise MI and copula $\rho$ are related by $\mathrm{MI}(i,j) = -\tfrac{1}{2}\log(1-\rho_{ij}^2)$ \citep{nelsen2006copulas}, yielding $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \rho_{ij}^2$. Substituting into Equation~\ref{eq:keff} gives the closed form $K_{\mathrm{eff}} = K - \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i}\sum_{j \neq i} \rho_{ij}^2$, which decreases from $K$ (fully independent) as pairwise correlations increase. Together, $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\eta_{\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel.
47	
48	\subsection{Aggregation Methods}
49	
50	Beyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}).
51	
52	\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\sum_{j=1}^K \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] \geq \lceil K/2 \rceil$ (baseline).
53	
54	\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \propto \mathrm{acc}_j$, $\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\sum_j w_j \cdot \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] > 0.5$.
55	
56	\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).
57	
58	\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\text{correct} \mid \mathbf{v}) \propto P(\text{correct}) \prod_j P(v_j \mid \text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.
59	
60	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
61	\label{sec:stat_framework}
62	
63	To disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:
64	\begin{equation}
65	\label{eq:regression}
66	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon),
67	\end{equation}
68	with $\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values. Standardized coefficients $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = \hat{\beta} \cdot \mathrm{SD}(\log x) / \mathrm{SD}(\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate per predictor across the 12 conditions.
69	
70	To assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.
71	
72	To test whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).
73	
74	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
75	\label{sec:robustness_method}
76	
77	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position are below the conventional 20-cluster threshold, making the max-$p$ aggregation important for conservative inference. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure. Details are in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}. As a sensitivity check, we exclude judges with clean accuracy below 0.5 and verify that $\eta_{\max}$ effects remain stable.
78
```

```text
1	\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}
2	\label{sec:threat_model}
3	
4	A \emph{panel} $\mathcal{P} = \{J_1, \ldots, J_K\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \in \{0,1\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\eta_{\max} = \max_j \eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \emph{panel attack success rate} is $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) = \Pr\!\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \geq t\bigr)$.
5	
6	To quantify diversity, we define the \emph{effective independence} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information among judges:
7	\begin{equation}
8	\label{eq:keff}
9	K_{\mathrm{eff}} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K} \Bigl[1 + \sum_{j \neq i} \exp\!\bigl(-2 \cdot \mathrm{MI}(i,j)\bigr)\Bigr].
10	\end{equation}
11	This formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\mathrm{MI} \to 0$) to $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\mathrm{MI} \to \infty$). Under the strong data-processing inequality (SDPI), the joint vulnerability satisfies $\eta_{\mathrm{joint}} \leq \min(1,\; K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max})$, suggesting that diversity should help, but this bound assumes non-adaptive faults.
12	
13	\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]
14	\label{def:adversary}
15	A \emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\{\eta_j(\cdot)\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}) = \sup_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.
16	\end{definition}
17	
18	This departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the SDPI bound implies.
19	
20	This suggests a \emph{reversal mechanism}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface; the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.
21	
22	\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}]
23	\label{thm:monotonicity}
24	Replacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\% of judge pairs (61/105); the remaining pairs lack uniform dominance, limiting direct applicability to a subset of substitutions.
25	\end{theorem}
26	
27	The theorem predicts $\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\ref{eq:regression}. The SDPI bound suggests $\beta_1 \leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal mechanism predicts positive $\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\eta_{\max}$ versus $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 4635 · 2026-05-23T12:55:02.421Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014VgAdtiFCFS2iHUnJn5gEb`</summary>

```text
1	% Mirror of abstract in main.tex — do not edit here.
2	
3	Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing inspiration from fault tolerance in distributed systems. We decompose panel vulnerability into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). We discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition: $\eta_{\max}$ dominates at small panels, but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ emerges as the dominant predictor at $K{\geq}5$ (bootstrap significance rising from 1/12 to 7/12). This aggregate positive association masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel and neutralizing both predictors at larger panels. The defense strategy must match the panel size regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence, with the net effect depending on attack type and aggregation method.
4	
```

</details>
